The definition of a normal family of meromorphic functions on a domain D can be extended
to include domains D that contain ∞. We say that a family 
 of meromorphic functions on a
domain D in the extended complex plane is a normal family of meromorphic functions on D if
 is a normal family on D\{∞}, and if further the functions g(w) = f(1/w), 1/w ∈ D, form a
normal family of meromorphic functions in some disk containing w = 0. Thus the notion of a
normal family is “local.” Marty’s theorem remains valid for domains containing ∞.

2. Theorems of Montel and Picard
As an application of Marty’s theorem, we give a proof of Montel’s theorem characterizing
normal families of meromorphic functions. Another proof of Montel’s theorem will be outlined
at the end of Chapter XVI, based on a modular function that arises as a covering map. We begin
with the following lemma. Since the converse is trivial, it actually provides a characterization
of normal families.
Theorem (Zalcman’s Lemma). Suppose 
 is a family of meromorphic functions on a
domain D that is not normal. Then there are points zn ∈ D converging to a point of D,
numbers ρn > 0 converging to 0, and functions 
 such that the scaled functions gn(ζ) =
fn(zn + ρnζ) converge normally to a nonconstant meromorphic function g(ζ) on ℂ satisfying
g#(0) = 1 and g#(ζ) ≤ 1 for ζ ∈ ℂ.

Suppose that f(z) is meromorphic for 0 < |z − z0| < r. A value w0 ∈ ℂ* is an omitted value
of f(z) at z0 if there is δ > 0 such that f(z) ≠ w0 for 0 < |z − z0| < δ. Thus w0 is not an omitted

Theorem (Montel’s Theorem). A family 
 of meromorphic functions on a domain D

===== PDF PAGE 270 =====
that omits three values is normal.
Since normality is a local property, we can assume that the domain D is the open unit disk
{|z| < 1}. By composing the functions in 
 with a fractional linear transformation, we can
assume that the omitted values are 0, 1, and ∞. Since the functions in 
 are then analytic and
nonzero on the unit disk, they have analytic roots of all orders. Let 
 be the family of all 2kth
roots of functions in

 is normal. The functions in 
omit the values 0, ∞, and all 2kth roots of unity.
We will argue by contradiction. Suppose 
 is not normal. Then 
 is not normal. Let
Gk(ζ) be the entire function from the Zalcman lemma, so that 
, and Gk is
a limit of restrictions of functions in 
, appropriately scaled. Since the functions in 
 omit
the 2kth roots of unity, so do their scaled restrictions, and from Hurwitz’s theorem (Section
VIII.3), so does any nonconstant normal limit. Thus Gk omits the 2kth roots of unity. Now,
Marty’s theorem shows that {Gk} is a normal family. Let G be any normal limit of a
subsequence of the Gk’s. Then G#(0) = 1, so that G is nonconstant, and by Hurwitz’s theorem
again, G omits all 2kth roots of unity, this for all k ≥ 1. Since G is an open mapping, G omits the
unit circle. Thus either |G| < 1 or |G| > 1. Applying Liouville’s theorem to G in the first case and
to 1/G in the second, we conclude that G is constant. This contradiction establishes the
theorem.
The following theorem is a substantial generalization of the Casorati-Weierstrass theorem.
Theorem (Picard’s Big Theorem). Suppose f(z) is meromorphic on a punctured
neighborhood {0 < |z − z0| < δ} of z0. If f(z) omits three values at z0, then f(z) extends to be
meromorphic at z0.
For the proof, we may assume that z0 = 0 and that f(z) omits the values 0 and ∞ on the
punctured disk. Then f(z) is analytic on the punctured disk. Let {εn} be a sequence that
decreases to 0, and define
Then {gn} omits three values, including 0 and ∞. By Montel’s theorem, {gn} is a normal
family. Passing to a subsequence, we can assume that gn(z) converges normally to g(z) for 0 <
|z| < δ. Assume first that g(z) is not identically ∞. Then g(z) is analytic for 0 < |z| < δ. Fix ρ, 0 <
ρ < δ, and choose M such that |g(z)| < M for |z| = ρ. Then for large n we have |gn(z)| < M for |z| =
ρ, and consequently, |f(z)| < M for |z| = εnρ. By the maximum principle, |f(z)| < M for εnρ ≤ |z| ≤
ρ, this for all large n. These annuli increase to a punctured neighborhood of 0 on which |f(z)| <
M. By Riemann’s theorem on removable singularities, f(z) extends to be analytic at 0, and the
theorem is proved in this case. If, on the other hand, g(z) ≡ ∞, then we apply the above
argument to 1/f(z). The argument shows that 1/f(z) extends analytically to 0, so in this case f(z)
extends meromorphically to have a pole at 0.
Theorem (Picard’s Little Theorem). A nonconstant entire function assumes every value
in the complex plane with at most one exception.
In other words, an entire function that omits two (finite) values is constant. To see this, we
regard ∞ as an isolated singularity of the function. The function omits two finite values and the
value ∞ at the isolated singularity at ∞. By Picard’s big theorem, ∞ is a removable singularity
or pole. Thus the function is a polynomial, and since any nonconstant polynomial assumes all
finite values, the function is constant.
Example. The entire function ez omits the value 0. The entire function sin z does not omit
any (finite) value.

the value 0 at ∞.

3. Julia Sets
One of the early applications of Montel’s theorem was to complex dynamics, the study of the
behavior of the iterates of an analytic or meromorphic function. Julia and Fatou used Montel’s
theorem as a key tool for studying the iterates of a rational function. One of their main ideas
was to understand the dynamical behavior of the iterates by splitting the extended complex
plane ℂ* into two invariant subsets, on one of which (the Fatou set) the iterates are well
behaved, and on the other of which (the Julia set) their behavior is chaotic. We will derive
some basic facts about Fatou and Julia sets in this section and the next.
Let U be a domain in the extended complex plane ℂ*, and let f(z) be an analytic map from
U to U. In other words, f(z) is an analytic function on U (meromorphic if ∞ ∈ U) whose range
is contained in U. For the remainder of this chapter it will be convenient to denote the nth
iterate f(f(⋯(f(z))⋯)) (n times) of f(z) by fn(z). Danger! This should not be confused with the nth
power f(z)n of f(z).
Example. If f(z) = z + 1, then fn(z) = z + n.
Example. If f(z) = λz, then fn(z) = λnz.
Example. If f(z) = zd, then fn(z) = zdn.
Example. If f(z) is a polynomial of degree d, then fn(z) is a polynomial of degree dn.
In general it is not possible to express the iterates of f(z) in a simple form. An important
method for obtaining effective information on the behavior of iterates is to make a “change of
variable” in order to express the function in a more tractable form, such as one of the forms
above, with respect to the new variable. Such a change of variable is called an analytic
conjugation. It is defined formally as follows.
Let f(z) be an analytic map from U into U, and g(ζ) an analytic map of V into V. We say that
f(z) and g(ζ) are conjugate if there is a conformal map ζ = φ(z) of U onto V such that
Thus φ ∘ f = g ∘ φ. We express this by saying that the following diagram commutes:
We can regard f(z) and g(ζ) as the same analytic function, expressed in different coordinate
systems. If φ conjugates f(z) to g(ζ), then φ also conjugates each iterate of f(z) to the
corresponding iterate of g(ζ),
Further, the inverse of φ(z) conjugates g(ζ) to f(z), φ−1 ∘ g = f ∘ φ−1.
Example. Every polynomial P(z) = Azd +⋯ of degree d ≥ 2 is conjugate to a monic
polynomial. Indeed, if we take ζ = φ(z) = cz and Q = φ ∘ P ∘ φ−1, we obtain

===== PDF PAGE 273 =====
We take c such that c1− dA = 1, and we have conjugated P(z) to a monic polynomial Q(ζ) of
degree d.
Example. Consider P(z) = z2 − 2. Let z = ψ(ζ) = ζ + 1/ζ be the conformal map from {|ζ| >
1} to the slit z-plane ℂ\[−2, 2]. We compute that
Thus φ = ψ−1 conjugates P(z) on ℂ\[−2, 2] to the map ζ ↦ ζ2 on the exterior of the closed
unit disk.
From the conjugation identity φ(f(z)) = g(φ(z)) we see that f(z0) = z0 if and only if g(φ(z0))
= φ(z0), that is, z0 is a fixed point of f(z) if and only if φ(z0) is a fixed point of g(ζ).
Differentiation of the conjugation identity yields φ′(f(z))f′(z) = g′(φ(z))φ′(z), from which it
follows that f′(z0) = 0 if and only if g′(φ(z0)) = 0. We say that fixed points and critical points
are “conjugation invariants.”
Example. Any fractional linear transformation f(z) with exactly two fixed points can be
conjugated to the multiplication g(ζ) = λζ for some complex number λ ≠ 0. Indeed, let φ(z) be a
fractional linear transformation that maps the fixed points z0 and z1 of f(z) to 0 and ∞,
respectively. Then ζ = φ(z) conjugates f to the fractional linear transformation g = φ ∘ f ∘ φ−1.
Since g(ζ) now has fixed points at 0 and ∞, g(ζ) must have the form λζ. A similar argument
shows that if f(z) is a fractional linear transformation with only one fixed point, then f(z) can be
conjugated to the translation g(ζ) = ζ + 1. In this case we take φ(z) to be the fractional linear
transformation that maps the fixed point of f(z) to ∞, some other specified point z0 to 0, and
f(z0) to 1. (See Exercise II.7.12.)
Now let f(z) be a rational function, regarded as an analytic map from ℂ* to ℂ*. The Fatou
set of f(z), denoted by 
, consists of all points z0 ∈ ℂ* that have an open
neighborhood W such that the restrictions of the iterates of f(z) to W form a normal family of
analytic functions on W. In this case, the Fatou set of f contains along with z all points in the
open set W, so the Fatou set is open. The Julia set of f(z), denoted by 
, is the
complement of the Fatou set, 
. Thus 
 is a closed subset of ℂ*.
Example. For P(z) = z2 we have Pn(z) = z2n. Thus the iterates {Pn(z)} converge normally
to ∞ on {|z| > 1} and they converge normally to 0 on {|z| < 1}. If W is any disk containing a
point z0 with |z0| = 1, then the iterates Pn(z) converge to both 0 and ∞ on nonempty open subsets
of W, so the iterates do not form a normal family on W. Thus the Julia set of P(z) coincides
with the unit circle, and the Fatou set of P(z) is the complement of the unit circle.

For simplicity we focus now on a monic polynomial P(z) of degree d ≥ 2. The main feature
of P(z) that we will exploit is its attracting fixed point at z = ∞. Since P(z) ∼ zd as z → ∞, there
is R > 0 such that |P(z)| > 2|z| for |z| ≥ R. The iterates Pn(z) then converge uniformly to ∞ on the
exterior domain UR = {|z| > R}. In particular,

The basin of attraction of ∞, denoted by A(∞), is defined to be the set of all z such that
Pn(z) → ∞. This occurs if and only if Pn(z) ∈ UR for large n. We denote by P−n(UR) the set of
z such that Pn(z) ∈ UR. This is the inverse image of UR under Pn(z), so it is open. Since A(∞)
is the union of the open sets P− n(UR), A(∞) is also open. Since Pk(z) tends to ∞ as k → ∞
uniformly on P−n(UR), each P− n(UR) is contained in

Theorem. Let P(z) be a polynomial of degree d ≥ 2. The basin of attraction A(∞) of ∞ is
an open connected subset of ℂ* containing ∞. The Julia set 
 of P(z) coincides with the
boundary of A(∞), and it is a nonempty compact subset of ℂ. Each bounded component of 
 is simply connected.
The proof is straightforward. First note that A(∞) is completely invariant, that is, P(A(∞))
⊆ A(∞) and P−1(A(∞)) ⊆ A(∞). Thus P(∂A(∞)) is disjoint from A(∞), and P(∂A(∞)) ⊆ ∂A(∞).
Then also Pn(∂A(∞)) ⊆ ∂A(∞) for all n ≥ 1. In particular, the iterates Pn(z) are uniformly
bounded on ∂A(∞). By the maximum principle, the iterates Pn(z) are uniformly bounded on the
bounded components of the complement ℂ\∂A(∞) of ∂A(∞). Thus no bounded component of ℂ
\∂A(∞) is iterated to ∞, and A(∞) consists of just one connected component, the unbounded
component of ℂ\∂A(∞). By Montel’s theorem, the iterates Pn(z) form a normal family on each
bounded component of the open set ℂ\∂A(∞), so that all these components belong to the Fatou
set 
, and the Julia set 
 is contained in ∂A(∞). If z0 ∈ ∂A(∞), and U is any open disk
containing z0, then {Pn(z)} converges normally to ∞ on U ∩ A(∞), while the sequence is
bounded at z0. Thus no subsequence of {Pn(z)} can converge normally on U, and so 
.
Thus 
. Since the polynomial P(z) has degree d ≥ 2, it has at least one fixed point
z0 ∈ ℂ. Evidently, z0 ∉ A(∞), so A(∞) ≠ ℂ*, and 
 is not empty. Finally, since
A(∞) is connected, also A(∞) ∪ ∂A(∞) is connected, and each bounded component of the Fatou
set is simply connected. (See Section VIII.8.) This completes the proof.
Example. For P(z) = z2, the basin of attraction of ∞ is the exterior of the unit circle, A(∞) =
{|z| > 1}. The other component of the Fatou set is the open unit disk, which is the basin of
attraction of the fixed point 0.
Example. Let P(z) = z2 −2. We have seen that Pn(z) → ∞ for all z ∈ ℂ\[−2, 2]. On the
other hand, if −2 ≤ x ≤ 2, then −2 ≤ P(x) ≤ 2, so the interval [−2, 2] is invariant under P(z) and
disjoint from A(∞). Thus A(∞) = ℂ*\[−2, 2], and the Julia set is

These examples of “smooth” Julia sets are the exception rather than the rule. It turns out that
the only values of c for which the Julia set of z2 + c lies on a smooth curve are the values c = 0
and c = −2 treated above. Several filled-in Julia sets for other values of the parameter c are
depicted below. It can be shown that the Julia set of z2 − 0.6 (next page) is a simple closed
Jordan curve that is nowhere differentiable. This is true of all values of c in the “principal
cardioid of the Mandelbrot set,” except c = 0. We will show in the next section that the Julia set
of z2 + 0.251 (see below) is totally disconnected, that is, it contains no continuum.

The theorem above forms the basis for a method for producing computer images of Julia
sets, called the boundary scanning method. Fix R large, fix N large, and fix a grid of points in
the z-plane. For each point z in the grid, we compute the iterates Pk(z) until we reach an integer
n = n(z) such that |Pn(z)| > R, or until we reach k = N, in which case we set n(z) = N.
If we color blue those points z for which n(z) < N and we color green those points for
which n(z) = N, we obtain a picture of the filled-in Julia set, which is the union of 
 and the
bounded components of the Fatou set. The integer n(z) measures the escape rate of z to ∞. The
escape rates can be captured visually by using different colors or shadings for different ranges
of n(z). An image of the Julia set can be obtained by recoloring red all those grid points for
which n(z) = N but that have a close neighbor satisfying n(z) < N.

This theorem provides another method for producing computer images of Julia sets, called
the inverse iteration method. One selects a starting point

inverse iterates P−k(z1) by solving the polynomial equations Pk(z) = z1 for 1 ≤ k ≤ N. For this
method to be effective, the degree of P(z) should be small (as d = 2). For a starting point in

one can look among the fixed points of f(z). A rational function of degree d ≥ 2 always has a
fixed point in the Julia set (see Exercises 16 and 23).
The preceding theorem provides the basis for referring to Julia sets as “fractal sets,” in the
sense that shapes in the Julia set reappear infinitely often at different scales near any point of
the Julia set. The idea is as follows. Let

contain any of the critical points of P(z), and let z1 be any point of 
. By the theorem, we can
find images Pn(z0) arbitrarily close to z1. If U is a small enough disk centered at z0, then Pn(z)
maps U conformally onto an open set containing Pn(z0). Since 
 is completely invariant, Pn(z)
maps 
 onto 
. Hence

 though at a
different scale.

A point z0 is a periodic point of f(z) if it is a fixed point of fm(z) for some m ≥ 1. For such

we define the multiplier of the cycle {z0, z1,..., zm −1} to be the multiplier of fm(z) at any of
the points of the cycle. The cycle is an attracting cycle if its multiplier λ satisfies |λ| < 1,
and it is a repelling cycle if |λ| > 1. The integer m is the period of the periodic point, or the
length of the cycle.

4. Connectedness of Julia Sets
The critical points and their forward orbits play a special role in complex dynamics. In this
section we prove two results on connectedness of Julia sets, in which the critical points play
the crucial role. While we focus on polynomials, the main ideas of the proofs are flexible and
can be adapted to other situations (see the exercises).
We consider a monic polynomial 
 of degree d ≥ 2. Let R > 0 be
large, so that the circle Γ0 = {|z| = R} is contained in the basin of attraction A(∞) of ∞, and
|P(z)| > R for |z| ≥ R. We may assume that Γ1 = P−1(Γ0) is “almost” a circle of radius R1/d,
which is mapped d-to-one by P(z) onto Γ0. Let U0 be the “annular” domain between Γ0 and Γ1,
and define Γk =P−k(Γ0) and Uk = P−k(U0). Then each Uk is an open set with boundary Γk ∪ Γk
+ 1, and P(z) maps Uk d-to-one onto Uk−1 (counting multiplicity at critical points).
If z ∈ A(∞), then either |z| > R, or there is a first integer k ≥ 0 such that |Pk + 1(z)| > R. In the
latter case, Pk(z) belongs to U0 ∪ Γ0, and z ∈ Uk ∪ Γk. Thus A(∞) is the disjoint union of the

===== PDF PAGE 279 =====
exterior disk {|z| > R}, the open sets Uk, and the curves Γk separating Uk and Uk −1. The effect
of P is to map each Γk to Γk −1 and each Uk to Uk −1.
Since there are only finitely many critical points, we can arrange by adjusting R that no
critical point of P(z) lies on a curve Γk. Then P(z) is conformal at each point of Γk and maps
Γk d-to-one onto Γk −1. The composition Pk(z) is then conformal at each point of Γk and maps
Γk dk-to-one onto the circle Γ0. Thus each Γk is a finite union of disjoint simple closed analytic
curves. The Γk’s accumulate on ∂A(∞) as k → ∞, and since 
, we have
Let Wk = P− k({|z| < R}). The boundary of Wk is Γk, and Wk is obtained from the disk {|z| <
R} by removing U0 ∪ Γ1 ∪ ⋯ ∪ Uk −1 ∪ Γk. Since the complement of Wk is connected, each
component of Wk is simply connected. In view of the Riemann mapping theorem, we can think
of Wk as the union of a finite number of “analytic disks” with analytic boundaries, one domain
for each closed curve in Γk. Since ℂ\Wk ⊂ A(∞), Wk contains all the bounded components of
the Fatou set 
 together with the Julia set 
. Note also that P−1(Wk) = Wk + 1 ⊂ Wk. Now the
stage is set, and we state and prove our two theorems.
Theorem. Let P(z) be a polynomial of degree d ≥ 2. The Julia set 
 of P(z) is connected
if and only if the iterates of each critical point of P(z) are bounded.
Suppose first that the iterates of critical points are all bounded, so that no critical point of
P(z) is in A(∞). Then at each point w = P(z) ∈ A(∞) we can define d analytic branches of the
inverse function P−1(w), and these branches can be continued analytically along any curve in
A(∞). Similarly, there are dk analytic branches of the inverse P−k(w) of the kth iterate Pk(z),
and P−k(w) can be continued analytically along any path in A(∞). We deform Γ0 to Γ1 through a
family of circular closed disjoint curves Γt in U0, 0 ≤ t ≤ 1. (In fact, since U0 is conformally
equivalent to an annulus, we can arrange for the Γt’s to be simple closed analytic curves.)
Suppose Γk consists of just one closed curve. Then as w traverses the closed curve Γ0 dk times,
an analytic branch of P− k(w) traverses Γk once and returns to its initial point. We deform the
curve Γ0 through the Γt’s to Γ1, and we follow a branch of P− k(w) dk times around each Γt.
Since there are no critical points in A(∞), the branch of P− k(w) moves continuously with the
curve Γt and cannot return to the initial value until w completes dk full loops around Γt. Hence
each P− k(Γt) consists of a single closed curve, and in particular, Γk + 1 = P− k(Γ1) consists of a
single closed curve. By induction on k we see, then, that each Γn consists of one closed curve,
and in particular, each Γn is connected. Since a limit of compact connected sets is connected
(Exercise 7), and since 
 is the limit of the connected sets Γn, 
 is connected.

===== PDF PAGE 280 =====
To prove the converse, suppose next that there is a critical point in A(∞). Let k be the first
integer such that there is a critical point in Uk, and for simplicity assume that there is only one
critical point q in Uk. The argument in the preceding paragraph shows that each Γj consists of
just one curve for j ≤ k. As before, we consider the circular curves Γt and their inverse images
P− k(Γt). Let r be the smallest parameter value such that q ∈ P− k(Γr). We can assume that Γt is
a circle for t near r. Fix ε > 0 small. The argument in the preceding paragraph shows that P−
k(Γr − ε) consists of just one curve, which is the boundary of a bounded simply connected
domain V containing q. Consider the inverse image P− k(Γr), which includes the critical point
of q. If the critical point has order m, then the part of P− k(Γr) near q consists of 2m + 2 analytic
arcs that terminate at q and divide a disk centered at q into 2m + 2 sector-like domains, as in
the figure. In the sectors we alternately have Pk(z) outside Γr and inside Γr. The curve P− k(Γr −
ε) = ∂V enters and then exits every other sector, as in the figure. The curves in P− k(Γr + ε) enter
and then exit each of the remaining sectors. We can think of V as a disk, via the Riemann
mapping theorem. Line segments drawn from q to the nearest point in ∂V in each of the m + 1
sectors where ∂V approaches q then divide V into m + 1 components. Since each of these
components contains an arc of P− k(Γr + ε), we see that P − k(Γr + ε) consists of at least m + 1
analytic curves. Hence Wk + 1 has at least m + 1 components. Since each of these components
contains curves in Γj for all j > k + 1, each of these components also contains points of 
, and
the Julia set is not connected.
We say that a compact subset K of ℂ is totally disconnected if for every z0 ∈ K and ε > 0
there is a subset E of K such that z0 ∈ E, the diameter of E is less than ε, and E is at a positive
distance from K\E. Thus K = E ∪ (K\E) is a decomposition of K into two disjoint compact
subsets, one of which contains z0 and has small diameter. At the opposite end of the spectrum
from the preceding theorem, we now have the following.

The theorem suggests an algorithm for obtaining computer pictures of

Mandelbrot set is approximated by the black grid points. By using different shades of blue to
depict different escape rates, we obtain more attractive pictures. For an assortment of close-up
pictures of various parts of the Mandelbrot set and a discussion of algorithms for obtaining the
pictures, see the award-winning book of Peitgen and Richter [PR].

XIII
Approximation Theorems
Theodore W. Gamelin1
Department of Mathematics, UCLA, Box 951555, 90095-1555, Los Angeles, CA, USA
In this chapter we prove two fundamental theorems, one “additive” and the other
“multiplicative,” on prescribing zeros, and poles of meromorphic functions. The first is the
Mittag-Leffler theorem, which asserts that we can prescribe the poles and principal parts of a
meromorphic function. The second is the Weierstrass product theorem, which asserts that we
can prescribe the zeros and poles, including orders, of a meromorphic function. The theorems
are closely related. Both theorems are proved by the same type of approximation procedure,
which depends on Runge’s theorem on approximation by rational functions. We prove Runge’s
theorem in Section 1, followed by the Mittag-Leffler theorem in Section 2. In Section 3 we
introduce infinite products, which can always be converted to infinite series by taking
logarithms. We prove the Weierstrass product theorem in Section 4.
1. Runge’s Theorem
Suppose f(z) is a complex-valued function on a compact subset K of the complex plane. When
can f(z) be approximated uniformly on K by rational functions? When can f(z) be approximated
uniformly on K by polynomials (in z)? Any uniform limit of rational functions with poles off K
must be continuous on K, and further, since a uniform limit of analytic functions is analytic, it
must be analytic on the interior of K. These are necessary conditions for approximation. The
first nontrivial sufficient condition for approximation is given by the following theorem.
Theorem (Runge’s Theorem). Let K be a compact subset of the complex plane. If f(z) is
analytic on an open set containing K, then f(z) can be approximated uniformly on K by
rational functions with poles off K.
For the proof, let D be an open set with piecewise smooth boundary such that K ⊂ D and
such that f(z) is analytic on D ∪ ∂D. By the Cauchy integral formula,
We chop ∂D up into a union of short curves γj such that each γj is contained in a disk {|z −
cj| < rj} that is at a positive distance from K. Then f(z) = ∑fj(z), where
The function fj(z) is analytic off γj (Morera’s theorem) and vanishes at ∞. Each fj(z) has a
Laurent expansion in descending powers of z − cj that converges uniformly for |z − cj| > rj,
hence uniformly on K. Thus fj(z) is uniformly approximable on K by polynomials in 1/(z − cj).
Adding these approximants, we see that f(z) is uniformly approximable on K by rational
functions with poles off K.

===== PDF PAGE 287 =====
There is some flexibility with respect to the location of the poles of the approximating
rational functions.
Lemma. Let K be a compact subset of the complex plane, let U be a connected open
subset of the extended complex plane ℂ* disjoint from K, and let z0 ∈ U. Every rational
function with poles in U can be approximated uniformly on K by rational functions with
poles at z0.
Here we consider a polynomial in z to be a rational function with pole at ∞. The lemma is
established by a “translation of poles” argument. We define the set V to consist of those points ζ
∈ U such that 1/(z − ζ) is approximable uniformly on K by rational functions with pole at z0.
Thus if ζ ∈ V, then each power 1/(z − ζ)k is also uniformly approximable on K by rational
functions with pole at z0. Since z0 ∈ V, the set V is nonempty. Since U is connected, to show
that V = U, it suffices to show that V is open and closed in U. The “closed” assertion is easy. If
ζ ∈ U is a limit of a sequence ζj ∈ V, then 1/(z − ζj) converges uniformly on K to 1/(z − ζ), so
by the definition of V, also ζ ∈ V. The crux of the proof then is to show that V is open, and this
reduces to showing that if ζ0 ∈ V, and if ζ is near ζ0, then 1/(z − ζ) is uniformly approximable
on K by polynomials in 1/(z − ζ0). We begin with the special case ζ0 = ∞. We must show that if
ζ is near ∞ (that is, |ζ| is large), then 1/(z − ζ) is uniformly approximable on K by polynomials
in z. For this, we expand 1/(z − ζ) in a geometric series,
If |z| ≤ C for all z ∈ K, and if |ζ| > 2C, then the kth term of the series is dominated by 1/2k.
By the Weierstrass M-test, the series converges uniformly on K, and consequently, ζ ∈ V
whenever |ζ| > 2C. If ζ0 is finite, the proof is essentially the same, with a change of variable z ↦
1/(z − ζ0) to place ζ0 at ∞. We choose ε > 0 less that the distance from ζ0 to K, so that |z − ζ0| ≥ ε
for z ∈ K. If |ζ − ζ0| < ε/2, then |(ζ − ζ0)k/(z − ζ0)k| < 1/2k for z ∈ K, so the geometric series
is uniformly convergent for z ∈ K, by the Weierstrass M-test. Thus all satisfying |ζ − ζ0| <
ε/2 belong to V, and V is an open set. This proves the lemma.
By approximating with rational functions and then using the lemma to translate the poles,
we obtain immediately the following sharper version of Runge’s theorem.
Theorem. Let K be a compact subset of the complex plane, and suppose that f(z) is
analytic on an open set containing K. Let S be a subset of ℂ*\K such that each connected
component of ℂ*\K contains a point of S. Then f(z) can be approximated uniformly on K by
rational functions with poles in S.
In particular, if K is a compact subset of the complex plane, and if the complement of K is
connected, then each function analytic in a neighborhood of K can be approximated uniformly
on K by polynomials in z. There is a considerably more difficult theorem on polynomial
approximation, Mergelyan’s theorem, which asserts that any function that is continuous on K
and analytic on the interior of K can be approximated uniformly on K by polynomials in z. The
analogous statement for rational approximation is false.

===== PDF PAGE 288 =====

such that f(z) cannot be approximated uniformly on K by rational functions. We outline the
construction of such an example, known as a Swiss cheese set. Let S be a sequence that is
dense in the open unit disk . Select disks Dk = {|z − ck| < rk} by induction, such that the center
ck of Dk is the first point in the sequence S that is not contained in any of the closed disks Ej =
{|z − cj| ≤ rj} for j < k, and then choose rk so small that Ek is disjoint from the sets Ej for j < k.

There are many applications of Runge’s theorem to construct functions that do one thing or
another. We select one such theorem at random, in order to give an idea of how Runge’s
theorem can be applied. First we need a definition.

2. The Mittag-Leffler Theorem
Recall that if f(z) is a meromorphic function with pole at z0, and the Laurent expansion of f(z) at
z0 is given by 
, then the principal part P(z) of f(z) at z0 is the sum of the
terms with the negative powers, 
. Thus P(z) is a polynomial in
1/(z − z0), and f(z) − P(z) is analytic at z0. The Mittag-Leffler theorem asserts that we can
prescribe the poles and principal parts of a meromorphic function.
Theorem (Mittag-Leffler Theorem). Let D be a domain in the complex plane. Let {zk}
be a sequence of distinct points in D with no accumulation point in D, and let Pk(z) be a
polynomial in 1/(z − zk). Then there is a meromorphic function f(z) on D whose poles are the
points zk, such that f(z) − Pk(z) is analytic at zk.
Let Km be the set of z ∈ D such that |z| ≤ m and the distance from z to ∂D is at least 1/m.
Then Km is a compact subset of D, Km ⊂ Km+1, and each component of ℂ*\Km contains a point
of ℂ*\D. Let fm(z) be the sum of the functions Pk(z) for which zk ∈ Km+1\Km. There are only
finitely many such functions Pk(z). By Runge’s theorem, there is a rational function gm(z) with
poles in ℂ*\D such that |fm(z) − gm(z)| ≤ 1/2m for z ∈ Km. Then f(z) = ∑[fm(z) − gm(z)]
converges uniformly on each compact subset of D, by the Weierstrass M-test. Since 
 is analytic on Kn for N > n, and 
 has poles
at the points zk that are in Kn, with the prescribed principal parts, f(z) has the prescribed poles
and principal parts in D.
In the case that D is the entire complex plane ℂ, we can take Km to be the closed disk {|z| ≤

===== PDF PAGE 291 =====
m}. Then fm(z) is the sum of the functions Pk(z) for which m < |zk| ≤ m + 1, and we can take
gm(z) to be a partial sum of the power series expansion of fm(z) about z = 0.
Exercise. Find a meromorphic function on the complex plane whose poles are simple
poles at the positive integers with residues all equal to 1.
Solution. This corresponds to the principal part Pk(z) = 1/(z − k) at zk = k. Our first guess
is to try f(z) = ∑1/(z − k), but the sum does not converge. The constant term in the power series
expansion of 1/(z − k) about z = 0 is −1/k, so our second guess is to set gk(z) = −1/k and try
This series does converge, uniformly on bounded sets, by comparison with the series
∑1/k2. Indeed, if |z| ≤ R and k > 2R, then |z − k| > k/2, so the kth summand is bounded by 2/k2.
The technique of subtracting off terms of a power series expansion to guarantee
convergence can be used to construct doubly periodic functions with double poles. Let ω1 and
ω2 be two complex numbers that do not lie on the same line through the origin. We sketch the
construction of the Weierstrass P-function 
 associated with ω1 and ω2. This is a doubly
periodic meromorphic function on the complex plane, with periods ω1 and ω2, such that the
poles of 
 are double poles at the lattice points mω1 + nω2, −∞ < m, n < ∞, and the
principal part of 
 at mω1 + nω2 is 1/(z − mω1 − nω2)2. Again our first guess for
constructing such a function might be to sum these principal parts over all integers m and n, but
the sum does not converge. If we subtract the constant term of the power series expansion at z =
0, as in the preceding example, we are led to a series that does converge,
To prove that the series converges normally, we require an estimate for the number N(k) of
lattice points mω1 + nω2 in a fixed annulus {k < |z| ≤ k + 1}. We claim that N(k) ≤ ck for some
constant c. To see this, choose δ > 0 such that the distance between any two lattice points is at
least 2δ. We consider the N(k) disks of radius δ centered at lattice points in the annulus. Since
none of the disks overlap, they cover an area of πδ2N(k). We may assume that δ < 1, and then
these disks are all contained in the enlarged annulus {k − 1 < |z| < k + 2}, which has area 6πk +
3π. From πδ2N(k) ≤ 6πk + 3π we obtain the asserted estimate N(k) ≤ ck. If now |z| ≤ R, and if k
> 2R, then each summand with pole in the kth annulus is estimated easily by C/k3, where the
constant C depends on R but not on k. Since ∑N(k)C/k3 converges, the Weierstrass M-test
shows that the series defining 
 converges uniformly for |z| ≤ R. Hence the series converges
normally on ℂ to a meromorphic function. Further, the series converges absolutely at each z, so
that we may arrange the terms of the series in any order. For each fixed n the series
converges to a periodic function of z of period ω1. Adding the appropriate constants and
summing over n, we obtain 
. Similarly, 
, and hence 
 is doubly periodic.

for which 0 ≤ x ≤ 1. In this strip we have
By the Weierstrass M-test, the series for f(z) converges uniformly and f(z) is bounded for |y|
≥ 1 and 0 ≤ x ≤ 1. Since each summand tends to 0 as |y| → ∞, the uniform convergence implies
that f(x + iy) → 0 as |y| → ∞, 0 ≤ x ≤ 1. The identity |sin z|2 = |sin x|2 + |sinh y|2 shows that also
π2/sin2(πz) is bounded for |y| ≥ 1 and tends to 0 as |y| → ∞. Thus f(z) − π2/sin2(πz) is bounded
for |y| ≥ 1, hence bounded on the vertical strip {0 ≤ x ≤ 1}, hence by periodicity bounded on the
entire complex plane. By Liouville’s theorem it is constant. Since both f(z) and π2/sin2(πz) tend
to 0 as |y| → ∞, the constant is zero, and f(z) coincides with π2/sin2(πz).

3. Infinite Products
An infinite product is an expression of the form 
, where the pj’s are complex numbers.

===== PDF PAGE 294 =====
We say that the infinite product converges if pj → 1 and ∑ Log pj converges, where we sum
only over terms for which pj ≠ 0. If the infinite product converges, we define its value to be 0 if
one of the pj’s is 0; otherwise, we define it to be
Thus any question we might ask about infinite products can be translated to a question about
infinite series by taking logarithms.
To help clarify the definition of convergent infinite product, we make several simple
observations. First, if ∏pj converges, then at most finitely many of the pj’s can be 0. This is
because pj → 1. Second, if ∏pj converges, then
This is because exp(Log pj) = pj. Third, we can always factor out a finite number of terms
from a convergent infinite product,
Finally, if an infinite product converges, and none of the factors is 0, then the product
cannot be 0.
Example. Consider
Since (1 + 1/(2k − 1)) (1 − 1/(2k)) = 1, the product of the first m terms is equal to 1 if m is
even, and it is equal to the last factor 1 + 1/m if m is odd. Thus the product converges to 1.
It is often convenient to write pj = 1 + aj and to express the product as ∏(1 + aj). If the
product converges, then aj → 0, and only finitely many of the aj’s are equal to −1. For most
purposes we can ignore the terms for which 1 + aj = 0 and work with the “tail” 
of the infinite product, for which aj is near 0.
If 0 < t ≤ 1, we have the estimate t/2 ≤ log(1+t) ≤ t. From this estimate it follows that if tj ≥
0, then ∑tj converges if and only if ∑ Log(1 + tj) converges. This leads immediately to the
following test for convergence of infinite products.
Theorem. If tj ≥ 0, then ∏(1 + tj) converges if and only if ∑tj converges.
Example. The infinite product
converges for α > 1 and diverges for α ≤ 1.
The infinite product ∏(1 + aj) is said to converge absolutely if aj → 0 and ∑ Log(1 + aj)
converges absolutely, where we sum over the terms for which aj ≠ −1. If ∏(1 + aj) converges
absolutely, then ∑ Log(1 + aj) converges, and ∏(1 + aj) converges.
Since Log(1 + w) is analytic at w = 0 and has power series expansion 
, we see that |Log(1 + aj)| is comparable to |aj| when aj is near 0.
Consequently, ∑|Log(1 + aj)| converges if and only if ∑|aj| converges. This together with the
preceding theorem yield the following.

===== PDF PAGE 295 =====
Theorem. The infinite product ∏(1 + aj) converges absolutely if and only if ∑aj
converges absolutely. This occurs if and only if ∏(1 + |aj|) converges.
Example. We have seen that ∏(1 + (−1)k+1/k) converges. However, it does not converge
absolutely, on account of the divergence of the harmonic series, ∑1/k = ∞.
Example. Consider the infinite product ∏(1 + i/k). Since Log(1 + i/k) = 
, the
series ∑ Log(1 + i/k) does not converge, by comparison with the harmonic series.
Consequently, ∏(1 + i/k) does not converge. However, since 0 < Log |1 + i/k| = ½ log(1 + 1/k2)
< 1/k2, the infinite product ∏|1 + i/k| does converge. Thus absolute convergence of an infinite
product is not equivalent to the convergence of the product of the absolute values of the factors.
Now we turn to infinite products of functions. The Weierstrass M-test for a sum of functions
is converted easily to the following test for an infinite product of functions.

there are constants Mk > 0 such that ∑Mk < ∞, and |hk(x)| ≤ Mk for x ∈ E. Then

Note that the function G′(z)/G(z) has poles at the zeros of G(z). However, the hypothesis
implies that gk(z) → 1 uniformly on any compact subset of D, so the summands

affected by the first terms of the series, the poles do not affect the uniform convergence. Since

4. The Weierstrass Product Theorem
The Weierstrass product theorem is a companion theorem to the Mittag-Leffler theorem. The
Mittag-Leffler theorem asserts that we can prescribe the poles and principal parts of a

===== PDF PAGE 298 =====
meromorphic function. The Weierstrass product theorem asserts that we can prescribe the zeros
and poles of a meromorphic function together with their orders.
Recall that the order of a meromorphic function f(z) at a point z0 is the order of the zero if
f(z0) = 0, and it is minus the order of the pole if f(z) has a pole at z0. If z0 is neither a pole nor a
zero of f(z), the order of f(z) at z0 is defined to be 0.
Theorem (Weierstrass Product Theorem). Let D be a domain in the complex plane. Let
{zk} be a sequence of distinct points of D with no accumulation point in D, and let {nk} be a
sequence of integers (positive or negative). Then there is a meromorphic function f(z) on D
whose only zeros and poles are at the points zk, such that the order of f(z) at zk is nk.
The proof runs parallel to the proof of the Mittag-Leffler theorem. Let Km be the set of z ∈
D such that |z| ≤ m and the distance from z to ∂D is at least 1/m. Then Km is a compact subset of
D, Km ⊂ Km+1, and each component of ℂ*\Km contains a point of ℂ*\D. Suppose zk ∈
Km+1\Km. We connect zk to a point wk ∈ C*\D by a simple curve γk in ℂ*\Km. If wk ≠ ∞ we
define fk(z) to be an analytic branch of log((z − zk)/(z − wk)) in the simply connected domain
ℂ*\γk. If wk = ∞, we take fk(z) to be an analytic branch of log(1 − z/zk). By Runge’s theorem,
there is a rational function gk(z) with only pole at wk such that |fk(z) − gk(z)| ≤ 2−k/nk on Km.
We consider the product
where we replace the factor in parentheses by 1 − z/zk if wk = ∞ and zk ≠ 0 and by znk if
both wk = ∞ and zk = 0. Now nk(fk(z) − gk(z)) is not defined on γk. However, its exponential
is meromorphic on D and has order nk at zk and no other poles or zeros. By the Weierstrass
M-test, 
 converges uniformly on Km, where N is chosen sufficiently
large to exclude terms with zk ∈ Km. Hence the infinite product defining f(z) converges
normally on D to a meromorphic function. Clearly, f(z) has the desired zeros and poles.
In the case that D = ℂ is the entire complex plane, the points wk are all ∞. Suppose zk ≠ 0
for k ≥ 1, and let n0 be the order (possibly zero) of f(z) at z = 0. The product expansion then has
the form
where gk(z) is an approximation to Log(1 − z/zk) for |z| < |zk|. We can take gk(z) to be a
partial sum for the power series expansion of Log(1 − z/zk) at z = 0. Since

XIV
Some Special Functions
Theodore W. Gamelin1
Department of Mathematics, UCLA, Box 951555, 90095-1555, Los Angeles, CA, USA
Our aim in this chapter is to illustrate the power of complex analysis by proving a deep
theorem in number theory, the prime number theorem, which does not appear at first glance to
be related to complex analysis. Along the way we introduce various functions that play an
important role in complex analysis. In Section 1 we introduce the gamma function Γ(z), which
provides a meromorphic extension of the factorial function. We derive the asymptotic
properties of the gamma function in Section 2 by viewing it as a Laplace transform. This yields
Stirling’s asymptotic formula for n!. In Section 3 we study the zeta function, which is a
meromorphic function whose zeros are related to the asymptotic distribution of prime numbers.
In Section 4 we study Dirichlet series associated with various number-theoretic functions,
thereby giving a strong hint of the fecund relationship between complex analysis and number
theory. The proof of the prime number theorem is given in Section 5.
1. The Gamma Function
The gamma function Γ(z) is a meromorphic function that arises frequently in complex analysis.
It extends the factorial function from the positive integers to the entire complex plane. It is

2. Laplace Transforms
Our aim in this section is to derive an asymptotic approximation to Laplace transforms of
functions that do not grow too fast. This leads to an asymptotic approximation to Γ(z), thence to
Stirling’s formula giving an asymptotic approximation to n!.

It is natural to consider the asymptotic behavior of Laplace transforms in the right half-
plane as x = Re z → ∞. However, for some classes of functions it is more appropriate to
consider asymptotic behavior in a sector as |z| → ∞ rather than in a half-plane. Let f(z) be
defined and analytic in a sector S = {−θ0 < arg z < θ1}. We say that 
 is an
asymptotic series for f(z) in the sector S if for each N ≥ 0,
as z → ∞ in the sector. If ∑aj/zj is an asymptotic series for f(z), then
Hence the coefficients in the asymptotic series of f(z) are uniquely determined. We refer to
∑aj/zj as the asymptotic expansion of f(z) in the sector.
If f(z) is analytic at ∞, then its Laurent series is an asymptotic series for f(z) in any sector.
However, asymptotic series generally do not converge. (See the Exercises.) Further, it can
happen that different functions have the same asymptotic expansion. For example, since zne−z
→ 0 as z → ∞ in any sector {|arg z| < (π/2) − ε}, the function e−z has an asymptotic series in the
sector that is identically zero.
The asymptotic approximation theorem shows that if h(s) has at most exponential growth,
and if h(s) is infinitely differentiable at s = 0, then the Laplace transform ( h)(z) has the
asymptotic expansion ∑h(k) (0)/zk + 1 in any sector Sε = {|arg z| < (π/2) − ε}. In any such sector
there is an estimate of the form cε |z| ≤ Re z ≤ |z|; consequently, |z| → ∞ if and only if x = Re z →
∞. In general, the series need not be an asymptotic expansion for ( h)(z) in the entire right half-
plane, regarded as a sector. However, in the case of the function logΓ(z), it is. In fact, it can be
shown that the series (2.6) is an asymptotic series for Log Γ(z) in any sector {|arg z| < π − ε},
for any ε > 0.

3. The Zeta Function
The zeta function was first introduced by Euler. He derived an infinite product representation
for the zeta function that connected it to prime numbers. A century later Riemann discovered a
close relationship between the zeta function and the asymptotic distribution of prime numbers.
Except for certain “trivial” zeros on the negative axis, the zeros of the zeta function are easily
seen to lie in the “critical” strip {0 ≤ Re s ≤ 1}. Riemann related the zeros of the zeta function
to the asymptotic distribution of prime numbers by an “explicit formula,” and he conjectured
that the zeros in the critical strip all lie on the line 
. At the beginning of the new
millennium the most famous unsolved problem in complex analysis, if not in all of mathematics,
is to determine whether the Riemann hypothesis holds. We will show in the next section that the
zeta function has no zeros on the boundary lines of the critical strip, and this will lead to the
prime number theorem.
In this context it is traditional to denote the complex variable by s = σ + it. With this
notation, the zeta function is defined by
The series converges absolutely for σ > 1, and it converges uniformly for σ ≥ 1 + ε for any
ε > 0, by comparison with the numerical series ∑n−(1 + ε) The function ζ(σ) is a decreasing
function of σ for 1 < σ < ∞, and ζ(σ) → 1 as σ → +∞. Since the harmonic series ∑1/n diverges,
ζ(σ) → +∞ as σ → 1+. Note also that |ζ(σ + it)| ≤ ζ(σ) for all t. We have already met the series
for ζ(2),
For the remainder of this chapter, we will always use p to denote a prime number, p = 2, 3,
5, 7, 11,⋯. The following infinite product representation of the zeta function signals a
connection between ζ(s) and prime numbers.
Theorem. If Re s > 1, then
 
(3.1)
where the product is taken over all prime numbers p.
The series ∑1/pσ + it converges absolutely for σ > 1, and it converges uniformly in any half-
plane {σ ≥ 1 + ε}. Hence the product (3.1) converges. Consider the geometric series
If we multiply together the m series corresponding to primes p1,..., pm, we obtain
Since every integer n ≥ 1 has a unique representation as a product of powers of distinct
primes, a summand 1/ns appears at most once in this sum, so the sum is a subsum of the series
∑n−s. As we incorporate more primes into the product, we eventually capture all terms n−s, and

===== PDF PAGE 308 =====
in the limit we have

Theorem. The zeta function ζ(s) is a meromorphic function on the complex plane with
only one pole, a simple pole at s = 1 with residue 1.

The functional equation of the zeta function yields information on its zeros. On account of
the product representation (3.1), the zeta function has no zeros in the half-plane {Re s > 1}.
Since 1/Γ(z) has a product representation expressing it as an entire function, Γ(z) has no zeros.
The functional equation then shows that the only zeros of ζ(s) in the left half-plane {Re s < 0}
are the zeros of sin(πs/2), which are simple zeros at s = −2, −4, −6, .... As mentioned above,
these zeros are called the trivial zeros of the zeta function. The nontrivial zeros of the zeta
function lie in the strip {0 ≤ Re s ≤ 1}, which is called the critical strip. Many outstanding
mathematicians have worked on the Riemann hypothesis, that the nontrivial zeros of the zeta
function lie on the line 
. It is known that ζ(s) has infinitely many zeros in the critical
strip. Their asymptotic distribution is known, and it is known that at least a third of them lie on
the critical line 
. Further, the first zillion or so zeros are known to lie on the critical
line. Meanwhile, whether the Riemann hypothesis is true remains a famous unresolved
problem.

4. Dirichlet Series
A Dirichlet series is a series of the form
 
(4.1)
which we will often abbreviate to ∑ann−s. The prototypical Dirichlet series is the series ∑n−s
representing the zeta function. Dirichlet series are important in number theory. They were used
by Dirichlet to study the distribution of prime numbers in arithmetic progressions.
The modulus |ann−(σ + it)| = |an|n−σ of the nth term of the series depends only on the real part
σ of s = σ + it. The modulus decreases as σ increases. Thus if the series (4.1) converges
absolutely for s = s0, then it converges absolutely for all s satisfying Re s ≥ Re s0. By the
Weierstrass M-test, it also converges uniformly in the half-plane {Re s ≥ Re s0}, by
comparison with the numerical series ∑ |an|n− Re s0. Proceeding in analogy with the definition
of radius of convergence of a power series, we define σa to be the infimum of σ for which ∑
|an|n−σ converges, and we have the following.
Theorem. For each Dirichlet series (4.1), there is a unique extended real number σa, −∞
≤ σa ≤ +∞, such that the series converges absolutely if Re s > σa, and the series does not
converge absolutely if Re s < σa. For any σ0 > σa, the series converges uniformly on the half-
plane {Re s ≥ σ0}. The sum f(s) = ∑ann−s is analytic for Re s > σa.
The extended real number σa is called the abscissa of absolute convergence of the series
(4.1).
Example. The series ∑n−s for ζ(s) has abscissa of absolute convergence σa = 1. It does not
converge at s = 1.
Suppose the function f(s) is represented by a Dirichlet series, say f(s) = ∑ann−s for Re s >
σa. Since the series converges uniformly in some half-plane, and each of the terms ann−s → 0
as σ → ∞ for n ≥ 2, we have
Consequently, f(s) → a1 as σ → ∞. Similarly, we can capture the other coefficients of the
series from f(s). We write
 
(4.2)
To estimate the series on the right, choose σ0 large so that ∑|ak|k−σ0 = M < ∞. Then
Since nσ/(n + 1)σ − σ0− 0 as σ → ∞, the series on the right-hand side of (4.2) tends to 0 as σ

===== PDF PAGE 312 =====
→ ∞, and the left-hand side of (4.2) tends to an as σ → ∞. Thus starting with a1, we can
determine in succession the coefficients an from f(s). In particular, the coefficients an are
determined uniquely by the function f(s), and we have proved the first statement of the
following theorem.
Theorem. If an analytic function f(s) can be represented by a Dirichlet series in some
half-plane {Re s > σ0}, then that Dirichlet series is unique. If f(s) = ∑ann−s and g(s) = ∑bnn
−s are represented by Dirichlet series in a half-plane, then so are their sum f(s) + g(s) and
their product f(s)g(s). Further, the Dirichlet series of the product f(s)g(s) is ∑cnn−s, where
Here the notation “d|n” means “d divides n.” The theorem follows by multiplying the series
for f(s) and g(s) term by term and regrouping the terms, which we can do for absolutely
convergent series.
The relation between convergence and absolute convergence of Dirichlet series is more
delicate than the corresponding relationship for power series. It may occur that a Dirichlet
series converges not only for σ > σa, but also for σ > σa − 1.
Example. The Dirichlet series ∑(−1)nn−s has σa = 1. However, it converges whenever s =
σ is real and positive, by the alternating series test. The following theorem shows that it
converges at every point of the right half-plane {Re s > 0}.
Theorem. If the Dirichlet series ∑ann−s converges at a point s = s0, then it converges for
all s such that Re s > Re s0. Further, it converges uniformly in any sector {| arg(s − s0)| ≤
(π/2) − ε} with vertex s0 and aperture strictly less than π.
This is proved using Abel’s formula for summation by parts. Let ∑ak be a series, with
partial sums
The formula for summation by parts is analogous to the formula for integration by parts,
 
(4.3)
This can be checked by substituting ak = Ak − Ak−1 and adjusting the indices.
Before applying the formula, we make two preliminary reductions in order to simplify the
proof. First, observe that ∑ann−s converges at s = s1 if and only if ∑(ann−s0)n−s converges at s
= s1 − s0. Thus by replacing ∑ann−s by ∑bnn−s, where bn = ann−s0, we can assume that s0 = 0
and that ∑an converges. The second reduction is to replace a1 by a1 − ∑an, so that we can
assume that ∑an = 0. We must show that for any C > 0, the series ∑ann−s converges uniformly
for |s| ≤ Cσ, where s = σ + it and σ > 0. We do this by invoking the summation by parts formula,
which in this case becomes

The preceding theorem allows us to define the abscissa of convergence of a Dirichlet
series ∑ann−s to be the extended real number σc, −∞ ≤ σc ≤ +∞, such that the series converges
if Re s > σc and does not converge if Re s < σc. Clearly, σc ≤ σa. It is easy to see (Exercise 3)
that σa ≤ σc + 1, and further, this estimate is sharp.
Example. Consider the series representation from Exercise 3.4,
 
(4.6)
The abscissa of absolute convergence of the series on the right is σa = 1. By the alternating
series test, the series converges for any positive real number s > 0. Consequently, the abscissa
of convergence is σc = 0, and the series converges in the right half-plane to an analytic function.
By the uniqueness principle, the identity (4.6) then holds in the entire right half-plane.

5. The Prime Number Theorem
We say that two functions f(x) and g(x) are asymptotic as x → ∞, written f ∼ g, if f(x)/g(x) →
1 as x → ∞. Let π(x) be the number of prime numbers p such that p ≤ x. Thus π(2) = 1, π(6) = 3,
π(10) = 4, and so on. The prime number theorem gives the asymptotic behavior of π(x) as x →
∞.
Theorem (Prime Number Theorem). The number π(x) of prime numbers not exceeding x
satisfies π(x) ∼ x/log x as x → ∞.
This formula for the asymptotic distribution of primes was surmised by several outstanding
mathematicians, including Gauss. Tables of factorizations of the first several hundred thousand
positive integers were already published before 1700. Gauss (or, more accurately, his
assistant) calculated all primes up to about 3,000,000, and Gauss used the results to aid his
researches into number theory.
After much effort by many mathematicians, the prime number theorem was proved in 1896
independently by Hadamard and de la Vallée Poussin. Their proofs were very difficult.
Subsequently, several new proofs of the prime number theorem have been given, and the
various proofs have been gradually simplified. We will follow D. Zagier’s exposition of a

===== PDF PAGE 316 =====
proof due to D.J. Newman (1972). As with most proofs of the prime number theorem, the proof
is divided into two parts. The first is to show that the zeta function has no zeros on the line {Re
s = 1}. The second is to establish a “Tauberian theorem” relating the zeta function and the
distribution of primes.
An important role in the proof is played by the function Φ(s) defined by the Dirichlet series
 
(5.1)
where as usual we use p for prime numbers. The series converges absolutely, and Φ(s) is
analytic for Re s > 1. If we differentiate logarithmically the product formula (3.1) for the zeta
function, we obtain
 
(5.2)
The series on the right converges to an analytic function for 
. Since ζ′(s)/ζ(s) is
meromorphic on the entire complex plane, (5.2) shows that Φ(s) extends to be meromorphic for
, with simple poles at the poles and zeros of the zeta function. Thus Φ(s) has a
simple pole at s = 1 with residue −m, if Φ(s) has a simple pole at s = s0 with residue 1, and
ζ(s) has a zero at s = s0 of order m.
Theorem. The meromorphic function Φ(s) has no poles on the vertical line {Re s = 1}
except at s = 1. The zeta function ζ(s) has no zeros on the line {Re s = 1}.
The proof is by magic. Fix t > 0, and suppose that ζ(s) has order q at 1 + it and order q′ at 1
+ 2it, where q, q′ ≥ 0. Then it has orders q and q′ at 1 − it, and 1 − 2it, respectively. Consider
Multiplying by ε(log p)/p1+ε and summing over the primes, we obtain
Since the residues of Φ(s) at the points 1, 1±it, 1±2it are respectively 1, −q, −q′, we have
εΦ(1+ε) → 1, εΦ(1 + ε±it) → −q, and εΦ(1 + ε ± 2it) → −q′ as ε → 0. In the limit we then
obtain
Since q and q′ are nonnegative integers, this can occur only when q = 0. Thus 1 + it is not a
zero of ζ(s) nor a pole of Φ(s). It follows that Φ(s) has no poles on the line 1 + iℝ except s = 1,
and ζ(s) has no zeros on the line.

XV
The Dirichlet Problem
Theodore W. Gamelin1
Department of Mathematics, UCLA, Box 951555, 90095-1555, Los Angeles, CA, USA
In Chapter X we used the Poisson kernel to solve the Dirichlet problem for the unit disk. In this
chapter we study the Dirichlet problem for more general domains in the plane. The basic
method, due to O. Perron, is to look for the solution of the Dirichlet problem as the upper
envelope of a family of subsolutions. In Section 2 we introduce subharmonic functions, which
play the role of the subsolutions. In Section 3 we derive Harnack’s inequality, which provides
a compactness criterion for families of harmonic functions. Perron’s procedure for solving the
Dirichlet problem is developed in Section 4. We apply the method to sketch another proof of
the Riemann mapping theorem in Section 5. In Sections 6 and 7 we introduce Green’s function.
1. Green’s Formulae
In this section we will establish three fundamental formulae for dealing with harmonic and
subharmonic functions. All are consequences of Green’s theorem. We assume that the boundary
curves and functions we treat are smooth enough so that Green’s theorem is valid.
Let γ be a smooth curve in the plane. As before, we denote by n the unit normal vector to
the curve. If γ is parameterized by arc length, γ(s) = (x(s), y(s)), then n = (dy/ds, −dx/ds). We
denote the derivative of a smooth function v(x, y) in the normal direction by ∂v/∂n. Thus
Let D be a bounded domain with piecewise smooth boundary, and let u and v be smooth
functions on D ∪ ∂D. We consider integrals of the form
By Green’s theorem, this is equal to
Rewriting this in terms of the gradient and Laplacian operators, we obtain Green’s first
formula,
 
(1.1)

===== PDF PAGE 322 =====
If we interchange u and v and subtract, the gradient terms cancel, and we obtain Green’s
second formula,
 
(1.2)
We will be particularly interested in this identity when one or both of u and v are harmonic.
Then the corresponding Laplacian term is zero, and the formula simplifies. In the very special
case that u is harmonic and v ≡ 1, the formula reduces to ∫∂D ∂u/∂n ds = 0, which was used in
Chapter III.
Suppose v(z) is harmonic in a punctured neighborhood of a point ζ. We say that v(z) has a
logarithmic pole at ζ if v(z) − log(1/|z − ζ|) is harmonic at ζ. The following theorem provides a
version of Green’s second formula for functions with a logarithmic pole, which is analogous to
Pompeiu’s formula (Section IV.8) for the operator ∂/∂z. It is sometimes referred to as Green’s
third formula.
Theorem. Let D be a bounded domain with piecewise smooth boundary, and let u be a
smooth function on D ∪ ∂D. Let ζ ∈ D, and let v(z) be a harmonic function on D\{ζ} such
that v extends smoothly to ∂D and such that v(z) − log1/|z − ζ| is harmonic at ζ. Then
 
(1.3)
Note that by Green’s second formula, the right-hand side of (1.3) is zero if we replace v(z)
by a function that is harmonic on all of D. Thus we can add to v(z) any harmonic function
without affecting the right-hand side. We add the harmonic function −v(z) + log(1/|z − ζ|) to
v(z), and we see that it suffices to establish (1.3) when v(z) is replaced by log(1/|z − ζ|).
So suppose v(z) = log(1/|z − ζ|). We follow the idea of the proof of Pompeiu’s theorem.
(The picture is the same as the picture for the proof of Cauchy’s integral formula in Section
IV.4.) We excise a small disk centered at ζ from D and set Dε = D\{|z − ζ| ≤ ε}, and we apply
Green’s second formula to Dε. Taking into account Δv = 0 on Dε, we obtain
Here we have reversed the orientation of the circle {|z − ζ| = ε} to the usual
counterclockwise orientation, so now for z = ζ + reiθ we have ∂/∂n = ∂/∂r. The logarithmic
singularity at ζ is absolutely integrable,
Consequently, the area integral over Dε tends to the area integral over D as ε → 0. To
establish (1.3), it remains to show that
 
(1.4)

2. Subharmonic Functions
Let D be a domain, and let u(z) be a continuous function from D to the extended real line
[−∞,∞). We say that u(z) is subharmonic if for each z0 ∈ D, there is ε > 0 such that u(z)
satisfies the mean value inequality
 
(2.1)
If u(z0) = − ∞, this inequality is automatically satisfied. Note also that ε may depend on z0. This
definition of subharmonicity is the same as the definition of the mean value property (Section
III.4), except that “=” is replaced by “≤” and the function u(z) is allowed to assume the value
−∞.
Example. Any harmonic function is subharmonic. If u(z) is harmonic, then equality holds in
(2.1) for all r such that the closed disk {|z − z0| ≤ r} is contained in D.
Example. If f(z) is analytic, then log |f(z)| is subharmonic. More generally, if u(z) is
harmonic, or even just subharmonic, on the open set where u(z) > −∞, then u(z) is subharmonic.
Example. If f(z) is analytic then |f(z)| is subharmonic. In this case the integral representation
 and an obvious estimate yield (2.1) for |f(z)|.
Subharmonicity is analogous in many respects to convexity of functions of one real
variable. A smooth function χ(t) is convex if and only if χ″(t) ≥ 0. (See Exercise 9.) There is a
corresponding characterization of subharmonic functions in which the operator d2/dt2 is
replaced by the Laplacian Δ.
Theorem. A smooth real-valued function u(z) is subharmonic on D if and only if Δu ≥ 0
on D.
This follows from Green’s third formula (1.3), applied to the functions u(z) and v(z) =
log(ρ/|z − z0|) on a disk {|z − z0| ≤ ρ}, which we assume is contained in D. In this case, formula
(1.3) becomes
This shows that the mean value inequality holds for all z0 ∈ D and all sufficiently small ρ
> 0 if and only if Δu ≥ 0.
We list several simple consequences of the definition of subharmonicity.
Lemma. If u(z) and v(z) are subharmonic, then
u(z) + v(z) is subharmonic,
cu(z) is subharmonic for any constant c ≥ 0,
w(z) = max(u(z), v(z)) is subharmonic.
To check (iii), for instance, let z0 ∈ D, and suppose the mean value estimate (2.1) holds for
u(z0) and v(z0). Then since u(z) ≤ w(z) and v(z) ≤ w(z), both u(z0) and v(z0) are bounded by 
. Hence so is their maximum w(z0).
Both the maximum principle and the strict maximum principle hold for subharmonic

===== PDF PAGE 325 =====
1.
2.
3.
4.
functions. The proofs are virtually the same as those for harmonic functions.
Theorem (Strict Maximum Principle). Let u(z) be a subharmonic function on a domain
D. If u(z) attains its maximum at some point of D, then u(z) is constant.
To prove the strict maximum principle, one shows first that the set where u(z) attains its
maximum M is open. The proof of this given in Section III.5 carries over directly. It requires
only the mean value inequality (2.1) in place of the mean value equality. The rest of the proof is
easy. Since u(z) is continuous, the set {u(z) < M} is also open. Since D is connected, the set
{u(z) = M} must then be either empty or all of D.
Theorem (Maximum Principle). Let D be a bounded domain, and let u(z) be a
subharmonic function on D. If lim sup u(z) ≤ 0 as z → ∂D, then u(z) ≤ 0 for all z ∈ D.
This follows from the strict maximum principle. We can argue as follows. The “lim sup”
condition implies that the subharmonic function w(z) = max(u(z), 0) tends to 0 as z tends to ∂D.
If we define w(z) to be 0 on ∂D, then w(z) is continuous on the compact set D ∪ ∂D, so it
attains its maximum at some point z0. If w(z0) > 0, then z0 ∈ D. By the strict maximum
principle, w(z) ≡ w(z0) is constant, contradicting w(z) = 0 on ∂D. It follows that the maximum
of w(z) is 0, and consequently, u(z) < 0 on D.
The following corollary to the maximum principle is obtained by applying the maximum
principle to the function u(z) − v(z). It justifies the nomenclature “subharmonic.”
Theorem. Let D be a bounded domain, and let u(z) be a subharmonic function on D. Let
v(z) be a continuous finite real-valued function on D ∪ ∂D that is harmonic on D. If lim
supz→ζ u(z) ≤ v(ζ) for all ζ ∈ ∂D, then u(z) ≤ v(z) for all z ∈ D.
We conclude with a technique for constructing subharmonic functions that will play a key
role in Perron’s procedure for solving the Dirichlet problem.
Lemma. Let u(z) be subharmonic on a domain D. Let D0 = {|z − z0| < ρ} be a disk such
that D0 ∪ ∂D0 is contained in D and such that u(z) ≠ −∞ on ∂D0. Define the function v(z) on
D to be equal to u(z) on D\D0 and to be the harmonic extension of u|∂D0 on D0. Then v(z) is
subharmonic on D, and u(z) ≤ v(z) on D.
The proof is a simple application of the maximum principle. The function v(z) on D0 is the
Poisson integral of the restriction of u to ∂D0, as in Section X.1. It is harmonic on D0 and
attains the boundary values u(z) continuously on ∂D0. By the maximum principle, u(z) ≤ v(z) on
D0. Consequently, v(z) is continuous and satisfies u(z) ≤ v(z) on D. Evidently, v(z) satisfies the
mean value inequality at any point of D\D0, because u(z) does and u(z) = v(z) there. On D0, v(z)
has the mean value property, since it is harmonic there. Thus v(z) is subharmonic on D.

3. Compactness of Families of Harmonic Functions
The Arzelà-Ascoli theorem asserts that a uniformly bounded family of equicontinuous functions

===== PDF PAGE 327 =====
is compact. For a family of analytic functions, the Cauchy integral representation formula
shows that uniform boundedness already implies equicontinuity. Hence a uniformly bounded
family of analytic functions is compact. We can draw the same conclusion for a uniformly
bounded family of harmonic functions using the Poisson integral representation formula. The
Poisson integral formula for the unit disk is
 
(3.1)
where
 
(3.2)
The Poisson kernel is an infinitely differentiable function of x and y. We can differentiate (3.1)
under the integral sign with respect to x and y, and when we do, we obtain integral
representations of the derivatives of u(x, y) in terms of the function u (eiθ) and the derivatives
of the Poisson kernel,
with similar formulae for the other derivatives. These integral formulae allow us to estimate
the derivatives of u(x, y) in terms of the maximum of M of |u (eiθ)| on the unit circle,
and similarly for the other derivatives. Since each derivative of the Poisson kernel is uniformly
bounded on each proper subdisk of the unit disk, we obtain uniform bounds for each derivative
of u(z) in terms of the maximum of |u(z)| on the unit circle. (See also Section X.1, Exercises 8
and 9.)
The same procedure works if we replace the Poisson integral representation for the unit
disk by the Poisson integral representation for an arbitrary closed disk. We obtain thus the
following.
Lemma. If 
 is a family of harmonic functions on a domain D that is uniformly bounded
on each closed disk in D, then the partial derivatives of the functions in 
 of any fixed
order are uniformly bounded on each closed disk in D.
In particular, the gradients of the functions in 
 are uniformly bounded on any closed disk
in D, so the functions in 
 are equicontinuous. (See Section XI.2.) From the Arzehà-Ascoli
theorem and a diagonalization argument, we obtain the following analogue for harmonic
functions of Montel’s theorem.
Theorem. Let 
 be a family of harmonic functions on a domain D that is uniformly
bounded on each compact subset of D. Then every sequence in 
 
 has a subsequence that
converges uniformly on each compact subset of D.
Families of positive harmonic functions are also compact, provided that we allow +∞ as a
possible limit for a sequence of positive harmonic functions. This follows again from the
Poisson integral representation. This time it is the positivity of the Poisson kernel that is
crucial.
Theorem (Harnack’s Inequality). If u(z) is a positive harmonic function on the open unit
disk , then
 
(3.3)
The proof depends on the estimate

===== PDF PAGE 328 =====
We can always approximate u(z) by a dilate u(ρz), ρ < 1, and assume that u(z) extends
harmonically across the unit circle. Then u(z) is represented by the Poisson integral formula
(3.1). Substituting the estimates for Pr(θ) into (3.1) and using the positivity of u(z), we obtain
Since the average value of u (ei(θ − φ)) is u(0), this becomes (3.3).
The estimate (3.3) is sharp. It becomes an equality for the function u(z) = Re((1 + z)/(1 −
z)) at z = ±r.
If we scale and translate Harnack’s inequality to an arbitrary disk, of radius R > 0 and
center z0, we obtain
 
(3.4)
for all positive harmonic functions u(z) on the disk |z − z0| < R. The estimate (3.4) is also
referred to as Harnack’s inequality. It allows us to draw some striking conclusions. From the
left-hand inequality it follows that if {un(z)} is a sequence of positive harmonic functions on
the disk {|z − z0| < R} such that un(z0) → +∞, then un(z) → +∞ uniformly on any subdisk {|z −
z0| ≤ ρ}, ρ < R. From the right-hand inequality it follows that if {un(z0)} is bounded, then un(z)
is uniformly bounded on any subdisk {|z − z0| ≤ ρ}, ρ < R. If we combine these observations
with a connectedness argument, we obtain the following.
Lemma. Suppose {un(z)} is a sequence of positive harmonic functions on a domain D. If
there is z0 ∈ D such that un(z0) → +∞, then un(z) → +∞ uniformly on each compact subset
of D. If there is z0 ∈ D such that {un(z0)} is bounded, then {un(z)} is uniformly bounded on
each compact subset of D.
To see this, let U be the set of z ∈ D such that un(z) → +∞. The remarks preceding the
lemma show that both U and D\U are open. Since D is a domain, either U = D or U is empty. In
the case U = D, we can cover any compact set by a finite number of disks on which un(z) →
+∞ uniformly, and we see that un(z) → +∞ uniformly on compact subsets of D. If U is empty,
we cover any compact set by a finite number of disks, and we see that {un(z)} is uniformly
bounded on each compact subset of D.
If we combine the preceding lemma and the compactness theorem for families of harmonic
functions, we obtain immediately the following compactness theorem for families of positive
harmonic functions.
Theorem. Let 
 be a family of positive harmonic functions on a domain D. Every
sequence in 
 has a subsequence that either converges uniformly on compact subsets of D
to a harmonic function or converges uniformly on compact subsets of D to +∞.
The compactness theorem for positive harmonic functions can be applied to monotone
sequences of harmonic functions.
Theorem. Let {un(z)} be an increasing sequence of harmonic functions on a domain D.
Then {un(z)} converges uniformly on compact subsets of D, either to a harmonic function or
to +∞.
To see this, consider the functions vn(z) = un(z) − u1(z). For each fixed z the sequence

===== PDF PAGE 329 =====
1.

5.

{vn(z)} is increasing, hence convergent either to a finite value or to +∞. The preceding theorem
shows that {vn(z)} has a subsequence that converges uniformly on compact subsets of D, either
to a harmonic function or to +∞. It follows that {un(z)} also converges uniformly on compact
subsets of D.

4. The Perron Method
Let D be a bounded domain. Recall that the Dirichlet problem for a given continuous function
h(z) on the boundary of D is to find a harmonic function u(z) on D that has boundary values
h(z). Any such function u(z) is unique. However, the Dirichlet problem need not be solvable.
Example. Let 
 be the punctured unit disk, and let h(z) be the continuous
function on ∂D defined by h(z) = 0 for |z| = 1, while h(0) = 1. If u(z) is a harmonic function on
D such that lim sup u(z) ≤ h(ζ) for ζ ∈ ∂D, then for any ε > 0, u(z) ≤ ε log(1/|z|) for |z| = 1 and
also for z near 0. By the maximum principle, the estimate holds on all of D. Letting ε → 0, we
obtain u(z) ≤ 0 on D. Consequently, u(z) cannot assume the boundary value 1 at z = 0.

5. The Riemann Mapping Theorem Revisited
The Riemann mapping theory can be proved by solving a Dirichlet problem. This was
Riemann’s original strategy, though he could not solve the Dirichlet problem in the full
generality of simply connected domains. We will sketch a proof along the lines envisioned by

===== PDF PAGE 333 =====
1.
Riemann.
We say that a subset E of the extended complex plane ℂ* is a continuum if it is compact
and connected and has more than one point. If E is a continuum, then ℂ*\E is simply connected.
Conversely, if E is a compact set such that ℂ* \E is simply connected, and if E has more than
one point, then E is a continuum. (See Section VIII.8.) The following criterion allows us to
solve the Dirichlet problem for a simply connected domain.
Lemma. Let D be a domain, and let ζ0 ∈ ∂D. If ζ0 lies on a continuum in ℂ\D, then ζ0 is
a regular boundary point of D.
Proofsketch. Let E be the continuum in ℂ\D containing ζ0, and let ζ1 be any other point of E.
Since ℂ*\E is simply connected, log((z − ζ0)/(z − ζ1)) has an analytic branch on ℂ\E; call it f(z).
The set D0 = {|z − ζ0|/|z − ζ1| < 1/e} is a disk containing ζ0, and Re f(z) < −1 for z ∈ D ∩ D0.
Define h(z) = Re(1/f(z)) for z ∈ D ∩ D0. Then h(z) < 0. Since Re f(z) → −∞ as z → ζ0, h(z)
→ 0 as z → ζ0, and h(z) is “almost” a barrier at ζ0. The difficulty is that h(z) might tend to 0 at
other boundary points of D ∩ D0. The proof is completed by following the procedure in the
proof of Bouligand’s lemma (pp. 8–9 of Tsuji’s book; see the references), in which a genuine
barrier at ζ0 is constructed from h(z).
Corollary. Let D be a bounded domain. If ℂ*\D consists of finitely many continua, then
every point of ∂D is a regular boundary point.
Now we can prove the Riemann mapping theorem. Suppose D is a simply connected
domain and that ℂ*\D has at least two points. As in Section XI.3, we can use the logarithm
function (or the square root function) and a fractional linear transformation to map D
conformally onto a bounded domain. Indeed, let ζ0, ζ1 ∈ ∂D, and let f(z) be an analytic branch
of log((z − ζ0)/(z − ζ1)) in D. If w0 = f(z0) for some z0 ∈ D, then the image f(D) covers a disk
D0 centered at w0. Consequently, f(z) cannot assume any of the values in D0 + 2πi on D, so that
1/(f(z) − w0 − 2πi) maps D conformally onto a bounded domain.
So we assume that D is bounded, and we also assume for convenience that 0 ∈ D. We
solve the Dirichlet problem for the function log |ζ| on ∂D, and we obtain a harmonic function
u(z) on D such that u(z) tends to log |ζ| as z ∈ D tends to any point ζ ∈ ∂D. Since D is simply
connected, the function u(z) has a harmonic conjugate v(z) in D. Define
Then φ(z) is analytic on D, and |φ(z)| → 1 as z → ∂D. By the maximum principle, |φ(z)| < 1
for z ∈ D. Further, φ(z) has only one zero in D, a simple zero at z = 0. Fix w such that |w| < 1.
We apply the argument principle to the domain {|φ(z)| < 1 − ε}, where |w| < 1 − ε, and we see
that φ(z) − w has exactly one zero on D. Hence φ(z) maps D conformally onto the open unit
disk.
If we analyze this proof and compare it to the proof given in Chapter XI, we see that both
proofs depend on the idea of compactness of families of functions, but in other respects they
are quite different. In this proof, Harnack’s theorem on equicontinuity of uniformly bounded
families of harmonic functions replaces the thesis grade version of Montel’s theorem. These
two compactness theorems are essentially equivalent. The use of regular boundary points and
criteria for regularity replace the use of the hyperbolically expanding property of the square
root function, and these techniques are quite different.

6. Green’s Function for Domains with Analytic Boundary
We say that the domain D has analytic boundary if the boundary of D consists of a finite
number of disjoint simple closed analytic boundary curves. We will focus in this section on
bounded domains with analytic boundary. In this case, every point of ∂D is a regular boundary
point, and we can solve the Dirichlet problem for any continuous boundary function. Further,
any harmonic function on D that is constant on a boundary curve extends harmonically across
the boundary curve, by the Schwarz reflection principle.
So let D be a bounded domain with analytic boundary. Fix ζ ∈ D, and let h(z) = log |z − ζ|
for z ∈ ∂D. Then h(z) is a continuous function on ∂D, and the Perron solution 
 is a
harmonic function on D that assumes the boundary values log |z − ζ| continuously on ∂D. We
define Green’s function for D with pole at ζ to be the function g(z) on D\{ζ} given by
It satisfies
 
(6.1)
 
(6.2)
 
(6.3)
Occasionally, it will be convenient to denote Green’s function by g(z, ζ), to emphasize the
dependence on ζ. If h(z) is another function with the properties (6.1), (6.2), and (6.3), then h(z)
− g(z) is a harmonic function on D that tends to zero on the boundary of D. By the maximum
principle, h(z) − g(z) = 0 on D, and h(z) = g(z). Thus Green’s function with pole at ζ is
uniquely determined by the properties (6.1), (6.2), and (6.3).
Example. Green’s function for the unit disk 
 with pole at z = 0 is g(z) = log(1/|z|).
Example. Suppose D is a simply connected domain, D ≠ ℂ, and let ζ ∈ D. Let φ(z) be the
Riemann map of D onto the open unit disk 
 satisfying φ(ζ) = 0, and set g(z) = −log |φ(z)|.
Since |φ(z)| → 1 as z → ∂D, g(z) → 0 as z → ∂D. Since φ(z)/(z − ζ) is analytic and nonzero at
z = ζ, we see by taking logarithms that g(z) + log |z − ζ| is harmonic at z = ζ, and so g(z) has a
logarithmic pole at z = ζ. Thus g(z) satisfies (6.1), (6.2), and (6.3) above, and g(z) is Green’s
function for D with pole at ζ.
Lemma. Let D be a bounded domain with analytic boundary, and let g(z) be Green’s
function for D with pole at ζ ∈ D. Then g(z) > 0 on D, g(z) = 0 on ∂D, and g(z) reflects
harmonically across ∂D so that g(z) < 0 on the side of ∂D outside of D. Further, the
directional derivative of g(z) in the direction of the exterior normal satisfies ∂g/∂n < 0 on
∂D.
The positivity follows from the maximum principle. Since g(z) → +∞ as z → ζ, we can

===== PDF PAGE 335 =====
choose ε > 0 so small that g(z) > 0 for |z − ζ| ≤ ε. The maximum principle, applied to the
domain D\{|z − ζ| ≤ ε}, shows that g(z) ≥ 0 for all z ∈ D. The strict maximum principle
guarantees that g(z) does not assume a minimum value 0 at any point of D, so that g > 0 on D.
By the Schwarz reflection principle (Section X.2), g reflects harmonically across the analytic
boundary curves in ∂D and g < 0 on the side of ∂D outside D. Thus ∂g/∂n ≤ 0 on ∂D. Let z0 ∈
∂D, and let f(z) be an analytic function defined on a disk centered at z0 whose real part is g(z).
Then |f′(z0)| = |∇g(z0)|. Since the set {Re f(z) = Re f(z0)} consists of only one analytic arc
passing through z0, f′(z0) ≠ 0, and consequently, (∇g)(z0) ≠ 0. Since g = 0 on ∂D, (∇g)(z0) is a
nonzero multiple of the normal vector at z0. Thus ∂g/∂n ≠ 0, and we conclude that there is strict
inequality ∂g/∂n < 0 on ∂D. The lemma is proved.
Again let D be a bounded domain with analytic boundary, and let g(z) be Green’s function
for D with pole at ζ ∈ D. Let u(z) be any harmonic function on D that extends continuously to
∂D. Green’s third formula (1.3), applied to u(z) and to Green’s function g(z) with pole at ζ,
becomes
 
(6.4)
We have established Green’s formulae only for functions that are smooth across the
boundary, but in the case of an analytic boundary it is easy to argue (Exercise 8) that the
formula holds whenever u is only continuous up to the boundary.
We define harmonic measure on ∂D for ζ ∈ D to be the differential
 
(6.5)

whenever v ≥ 0 on ∂D. If we apply (6.4) to the function u(z) = 1, we obtain

It is straightforward to check (Exercise 2) that the harmonic measure for an arbitrary point
